## 2026-02-02 12:38:07

fix the ProjectsManagerWebProduction errors and Summary of Migration Issues

  The project was migrated from /Users/elic/Documents/Claude/AIDevPlatformProduction to /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/AIDevPlatformProduction but several things broke:

  1. Virtual Environments (All Services)

  Problem: Python venvs contain hardcoded absolute paths in their shebang lines and pyvenv.cfg pointing to the old location.

  Affected services:
  - services/user-service/venv
  - services/project-service/venv
  - services/git-service/venv
  - services/agent-orchestrator/venv
  - services/qa-service/venv

  Fix needed: Delete and recreate all venvs, or run sed to fix paths.

  ---
  2. Docker Compose State

  Problem: Docker has stale container references (e.g., c8688b04a264) that no longer exist but are still referenced in volumes.

  Status: Partially fixed - I got postgres and redis running manually.

  ---
  3. Python Version Incompatibility

  Problem: System Python is 3.14, original venvs used 3.9.6. Many packages (pydantic, asyncpg) don't have pre-built wheels for Python 3.13/3.14.

  Workaround: Use Python 3.13 with latest package versions (not the pinned ones in requirements.txt).

  ---
  4. Missing Dependencies

  When recreating venvs, the pinned package versions in requirements.txt don't work with Python 3.13. Need to either:
  - Install Python 3.11 via pyenv
  - Update requirements.txt to use latest compatible versions

  ---
  Current State
  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │        Service         │         Status          │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Frontend (3000)        │ Running                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ PostgreSQL (5437)      │ Running                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Redis (6383)           │ Running                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ User Service (8001)    │ Broken - venv needs fix │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Project Service (8002) │ Not started             │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘
  ---
  Recommended Fix

  Run a script to recreate all venvs with Python 3.13 and install latest compatible packages. Would you like me to create that script?

  1. One execution at a time — global concurrency lock
  2. No whole-picture context — each task gets only its own title + description as prompt
  3. No agent specialization — same generic Claude Code instance for every task type
  4. No agent memory between tasks — cache is cleared before each execution
  5. Dumb sequential chaining — AutoPilot doesn't learn from previous task results
  6. No progress bar at top level — progress is only visible inside the Feature detail panel"

- What should be measured

## 2026-04-29 23:15:31

/jonny — engage VP-R&D bible. Plan and implement this feature using your CodeBoard-first discipline and the pre/post checklist. Apply the audit gates (code review + security audit + regression test) before marking the work COMPLETED_WAITING_QA.

I need you to implement the following BUG:

**CB-1941: BUG: Status cascade to parent doesn't fire when last child is patched to CWQ**

## Task Description
**Symptom (observed 2026-04-29 19:52-19:54)**: After Eli batch-flipped 14 children of JM-F1 to CWQ at 19:52:21 — at which point ALL direct children were CWQ — the parent JM-F1 (FEATURE) stayed at IN_PROGRESS for at least 2.5 minutes until manual `PATCH /api/issues/<f1>` at 19:54:50 forced it to CWQ.

**Timestamp evidence (locked)**:
- `CMOI-191` (last blocking child) updatedAt: `2026-04-29T19:52:21.087041`
- `CMOI-1` (JM-F1 parent) updatedAt: `2026-04-29T19:54:50.616852` ← only updated when manual PATCH ran
- Gap of 2 minutes 29 seconds with no auto-cascade firing despite all children being CWQ.

**Expected per `backend/utils/db_queries.py:585`**: `cascade_status_to_parents` says *'When all children of a parent are in the target status (or DONE), the parent is updated to the target status.'* This should trigger on every issue PATCH that lands a child in CWQ — and the patch endpoint in `backend/api/issues.py:566` and the batch endpoint at `:745` both call `cascade_status_to_parents`.

**Possible causes** (need investigation):
1. `cascade_status_to_parents` may be checking only EPIC/STORY children of the parent and skipping TASK/BUG types — CMOI-191 is a TASK child of a FEATURE, which may be an unsupported case in the cascade query.
2. The cascade may have a stale read — checking child statuses BEFORE the just-PATCHed child's commit lands.
3. The batch endpoint loop may swallow cascade errors silently.
4. Cascade may have a type-restriction issue (FEATURE only checks EPIC children, not TASK/BUG direct children).

**Reproduction**:
1. Pick any FEATURE/EPIC/STORY with multiple children where exactly one child is BACKLOG
2. PATCH that one child to CWQ via either single PATCH or batch endpoint
3. Wait 30 seconds. Check parent status — should be CWQ; observe it's still IN_PROGRESS
4. Manual `PATCH /api/issues/<parent-id> {"status":"COMPLETED_WAITING_QA"}` works as workaround

**Acceptance**: PATCH/batch-update of any child that completes the cascade gate should auto-flip the parent's status within ~5 seconds without requiring a manual second PATCH.

**Owner suggestion**: `python-pro` to inspect cascade_status_to_parents query and the batch handler's loop semantics. Add unit test reproducing the exact scenario above.

**Priority**: MEDIUM — workaround exists (manual PATCH parent), but it's a discipline-undermining UX glitch since the cascade rule is documented as automatic.

**Related**: CB-1940 (CANCELLED — UX gap that surfaced the same underlying issue from the user's POV).

Please implement this task. When you're done, summarize what you did.

## Task Description
Add to `backend/services/rag_service.py`:

```python
async def embed_execution_summary(self, project_id, issue_id, issue_key, summary: ExecutionSummary):
    doc_text = f"[{issue_key}] Implementation Summary:\n{summary.summary}\n\n"
    if summary.architectureNotes:
        doc_text += f"Architecture: {summary.architectureNotes}\n"
    if summary.technicalNotes:
        doc_text += f"Technical: {summary.technicalNotes}\n"
    if summary.componentsModified:
        doc_text += f"Components: {', '.join(json.loads(summary.componentsModified))}\n"
    if summary.filesTouched:
        doc_text += f"Files: {', '.join(json.loads(summary.filesTouched))}\n"

    doc_id = self.generate_doc_id(issue_id, content_type="execution_summary")
    collection = self.get_collection(project_id)
    collection.upsert(
        ids=[doc_id],
        documents=[doc_text],
        metadatas=[{
            "issue_id": issue_id,
            "key": issue_key,
            "content_type": "execution_summary",
        }],
    )
```

Follow the existing embed_ai_context() pattern.

## Task Description
Add to `backend/services/rag_service.py`:

```python
async def get_implementation_context_for_qa(self, db, project_id, issue_id) -> str:
    from models import ExecutionSummary
    from sqlalchemy import select

    # 1. Get the issue's own ExecutionSummary from DB
    result = await db.execute(
        select(ExecutionSummary)
        .where(ExecutionSummary.issueId == issue_id)
        .order_by(ExecutionSummary.executedAt.desc())
        .limit(1)
    )
    summary = result.scalar_one_or_none()

    if not summary:
        return ""

    # 2. Build formatted context string
    parts = [f"## Implementation Summary\n{summary.summary}"]
    if summary.filesTouched:
        files = json.loads(summary.filesTouched)
        parts.append(f"## Files Modified\n" + "\n".join(f"- {f}" for f in files))
    if summary.componentsModified:
        comps = json.loads(summary.componentsModified)
        parts.append(f"## Components\n" + ", ".join(comps))
    if summary.architectureNotes:
        parts.append(f"## Architecture Notes\n{summary.architectureNotes}")
    if summary.technicalNotes:
        parts.append(f"## Technical Notes\n{summary.technicalNotes}")
    if summary.challengesFaced:
        parts.append(f"## Known Challenges\n{summary.challengesFaced}")

    return "\n\n".join(parts)
```

The `db` parameter is an AsyncSession. Import json at top of file.

Create `backend/services/documentation_generator.py` with class `DocumentationGenerator`.

Core method: `generate_from_execution(session, issue, project_path, db) -> ExecutionSummary`

2. Build AI prompt with: output lines, git diff, issue context
3. Call AI (Ollama first, Claude API fallback — same pattern as ai_service._generate())
4. Parse structured JSON response into ExecutionSummary fields
5. Persist to DB via SQLAlchemy
6. Update Issue.implementationSummary field
7. Index in ChromaDB via rag_service.embed_execution_summary()

AI Prompt should produce JSON with:
- summary: 2-3 paragraph markdown overview
- componentsModified: list of components/modules
- filesTouched: list of files from git diff
- linesAdded / linesRemoved: from git diff --stat
- architectureNotes: architectural decisions
- technicalNotes: implementation specifics
- challengesFaced: errors/retries from output
- lessonsLearned: noteworthy items

## Task Description
Add to `backend/services/rag_service.py`:

```python
async def embed_execution_summary(self, project_id, issue_id, issue_key, summary: ExecutionSummary):
    doc_text = f"[{issue_key}] Implementation Summary:\n{summary.summary}\n\n"
    if summary.architectureNotes:
        doc_text += f"Architecture: {summary.architectureNotes}\n"
    if summary.technicalNotes:
        doc_text += f"Technical: {summary.technicalNotes}\n"
    if summary.componentsModified:
        doc_text += f"Components: {', '.join(json.loads(summary.componentsModified))}\n"
    if summary.filesTouched:
        doc_text += f"Files: {', '.join(json.loads(summary.filesTouched))}\n"

    doc_id = self.generate_doc_id(issue_id, content_type="execution_summary")
    collection = self.get_collection(project_id)
    collection.upsert(
        ids=[doc_id],
        documents=[doc_text],
        metadatas=[{
            "issue_id": issue_id,
            "key": issue_key,
            "content_type": "execution_summary",
        }],
    )
```

Follow the existing embed_ai_context() pattern.

## Task Description
Add to `backend/services/rag_service.py`:

```python
async def get_implementation_context_for_qa(self, db, project_id, issue_id) -> str:
    from models import ExecutionSummary
    from sqlalchemy import select

    # 1. Get the issue's own ExecutionSummary from DB
    result = await db.execute(
        select(ExecutionSummary)
        .where(ExecutionSummary.issueId == issue_id)
        .order_by(ExecutionSummary.executedAt.desc())
        .limit(1)
    )
    summary = result.scalar_one_or_none()

    if not summary:
        return ""

    # 2. Build formatted context string
    parts = [f"## Implementation Summary\n{summary.summary}"]
    if summary.filesTouched:
        files = json.loads(summary.filesTouched)
        parts.append(f"## Files Modified\n" + "\n".join(f"- {f}" for f in files))
    if summary.componentsModified:
        comps = json.loads(summary.componentsModified)
        parts.append(f"## Components\n" + ", ".join(comps))
    if summary.architectureNotes:
        parts.append(f"## Architecture Notes\n{summary.architectureNotes}")
    if summary.technicalNotes:
        parts.append(f"## Technical Notes\n{summary.technicalNotes}")
    if summary.challengesFaced:
        parts.append(f"## Known Challenges\n{summary.challengesFaced}")

    return "\n\n".join(parts)
```

The `db` parameter is an AsyncSession. Import json at top of file.

  2. OpenAI Symphony — ~20k ⭐ — https://github.com/openai/symphony
  Linear board = control plane. Each ticket → autonomous agent → CI-green PR + walkthrough video before human looks.
  Apache 2.0, April 2026. Match 8/10.

  3. claude-flow / Ruflo (ruvnet) — 34.1k ⭐ — https://github.com/ruvnet/claude-flow
  Biggest Claude-native swarm. 100+ agents, hierarchical/mesh/adaptive topologies, Raft+Byzantine+Gossip consensus, shared
   memory. Engine, no Kanban UI. Match 8/10.

  4. ClawTeam (HKUDS) — 5.1k ⭐ — https://github.com/HKUDS/ClawTeam
  Real P2P agent messaging (ZeroMQ), leader spawns workers w/ dependency chains, tmux+web kanban dashboards. Most
  ambitious mesh. Match 8/10.

  5. Composio Agent Orchestrator — 6.7k ⭐ — https://github.com/ComposioHQ/agent-orchestrator
  Parallel agents owning full PR lifecycle incl autonomous CI fix + review-comment response. Web dashboard. Match 8/10.

  6. Emdash (YC W26) — 4.2k ⭐ — https://github.com/generalaction/emdash
  24 CLI agents, Linear/Jira/GitHub ticket-driven, worktree-per-task. Match 8/10.

  Honorable

  Conductor (Melty Labs, closed-source Mac), Sculptor (Imbue, containerized parallel Claudes), OpenHands 72k⭐, MetaGPT
  67k⭐ (stale), GPT Pilot 33k⭐ (dead — 11-role agent team, ideologically closest to CodeBoard but unmaintained).

  Gaps nobody fills (your moat)

  - FEATURE→EPIC→STORY→TASK→SUBTASK hierarchy — zero competitors have it native
  - QA Board as separate execution graph — no COMPLETED_WAITING_QA w/ QA agents anywhere
  - Status cascade up/down hierarchy — unsolved everywhere
  - Bug-as-first-class agent-executable issue — only CodeBoard
  - Real agent↔agent handoff tied to ticket dependencies — ClawTeam has P2P but no hierarchy

  Risk

  Symphony (OpenAI-backed, April 2026, momentum). Will add hierarchy soon. ~2-quarter window.

  direction?

  ---
  Multi-Agent Dev Framework Survey — 2026-05-01

  Survey of open-source multi-agent / mesh / swarm systems whose stated purpose is "given a request, build the thing
  end-to-end with a team of cooperating AI agents." Stars verified directly from GitHub on 2026-05-01. Anything older than
   2025-Q3 is flagged as stale.

  Scoring rubric (1–10 match against Eli's CodeBoard vision: agent mesh that talks, parallel role-based agents, full-stack
   generation, ticket-driven Kanban/Jira-style UI, hierarchical work breakdown, AI-execution-per-ticket).

  ---
  1. TL;DR — Top 3 Closest Matches

  1. Vibe Kanban (BloopAI) — match 9/10. Kanban board + parallel coding agents + per-task git worktrees + multi-agent (10+
   CLIs) + diff review + MCP for plan-decompose-into-cards. Closest public analogue to CodeBoard, except it stops at
  "ticket → one agent" and does not push hierarchical Feature→Epic→Story→Task. Note: repo shows sunsetting notice — team
  moving on. Architecture to learn from before it freezes. (repo)
  2. OpenAI Symphony — match 8/10. Treats Linear board as control plane, spawns one autonomous agent per ticket, agents do
   CI/PR/walkthrough proof-of-work and only escalate when they need human. Exactly Eli's "AI execution pipeline per
  ticket" pattern, but Linear-bound and Codex/Kata-only. Apache 2.0 reference implementation in Elixir. Released
  April 2026. (repo)
  3. claude-flow / Ruflo (ruvnet) — match 8/10. Largest Claude-native swarm OSS (34.1k stars). Hierarchical/mesh/adaptive
  topologies, 100+ specialized agents, consensus protocols (Raft/Byzantine/Gossip), shared memory/RAG, multi-provider
  routing. No Kanban UI but orchestration substrate is deepest in space. If CodeBoard is the interface, claude-flow is the
   engine others bolt to. (repo)

  Honorable mentions: Composio Agent Orchestrator (autonomous PR lifecycle, web dashboard), Emdash (24-CLI ticket-driven
  IDE w/ Linear+Jira+GitHub), ClawTeam (HKUDS — leader spawns workers with dependency chains, tmux+web kanban).

  ---
  3. Detailed Cards

  1. Vibe Kanban — BloopAI

  - Pitch: Kanban board + parallel coding agents per task; spiritual ancestor of CodeBoard.
  - Link: https://github.com/BloopAI/vibe-kanban
  - Stars / momentum: 25.7k / 2.6k forks. 2,070 commits. v0.1.44 Apr 24 2026. Repo carries sunsetting notice — being wound
   down; treat as "prior art frozen in late form."
  - Architecture: Rust backend (50.3%) + TypeScript frontend (46%). Each ticket gets workspace with own git branch,
  terminal, dev server. Multi-attempt model: same task can be run by multiple agents in parallel and diffs compared
  side-by-side. MCP server lets a "planning" ticket auto-decompose into child cards.
  - Comms: No agent↔agent message bus. Coordination via Kanban model.
  - Models / agents: Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, GitHub Copilot, Amp, Cursor, OpenCode, Droid, CCR, Qwen Code (10+).
  - Builds: Real refactors and feature work in user repos.
  - Killer feature: "Multiple attempts per ticket" — sample solution space across models, then merge winner.
  - UI: Kanban + diff review + browser preview + inline comments.
  - Maturity: Production-grade but going dormant.
  - Match score: 9/10.

  2. OpenAI Symphony

  - Pitch: Spec + reference implementation that turns Linear board into control plane for autonomous coding agents.
  - Link: https://github.com/openai/symphony
  - Stars / momentum: ~20k / 1.7k. April 2026. Apache 2.0. Elixir 95.5%.
  - Architecture: Watches Linear board, every task spawns isolated agent run, agent produces "proof of work" — CI status,
  PR feedback, complexity analysis, walkthrough video. v1.1 added Kata CLI runtime (Claude/Gemini pluggable).
  - Comms: Board state IS message bus.
  - Models: Codex by default, Claude/Gemini via Kata.
  - Builds: Real PRs. OpenAI claims 500% increase in landed PRs in pilots.
  - Killer feature: Agents must justify with CI green + walkthrough video before human looks.
  - UI: Linear (existing).
  - Maturity: "Engineering preview for trusted environments."
  - Match score: 8/10.

  3. claude-flow / Ruflo (ruvnet)

  - Pitch: Largest Claude-native multi-agent orchestration platform.
  - Link: https://github.com/ruvnet/claude-flow (renamed Ruflo Jan 2026 — npm/CLI keep claude-flow)
  - Stars / momentum: 34.1k / 3.9k. v3.6.10 Apr 30 2026.
  - Architecture: UI → orchestration (27 hooks) → swarm coordination → 100+ specialized agents → memory/learning →
  multi-provider routing. Topologies: hierarchical, mesh, adaptive. Consensus: Raft, Byzantine, Gossip. HNSW vector memory
   (AgentDB), WASM/Rust policy kernels, mTLS zero-trust federation. 32 native Claude Code plugins + 21 npm.
  - Comms: Real shared memory + federation protocol — most legitimately "agent mesh" of any project here.
  - Models: Claude (primary), GPT, Gemini, Cohere, Ollama with intelligent routing+failover.
  - Builds: Plumbing-level; you compose agents into apps.
  - Killer feature: Topology zoo + consensus algorithms — only OSS framework with Byzantine consensus over agents.
  - UI: CLI + Claude Code plugin. No first-party Kanban.
  - Match score: 8/10. Engine, not interface.

  4. Composio Agent Orchestrator

  - Pitch: Parallel coding agents that own full PR lifecycle including CI fixes and review responses.
  - Link: https://g

<result>I have enough context. Final audit findings below.

---

# Security Audit — E2 Frontend + E3 Backend (CB-2069 / CB-2091)

**Scope reviewed**: `FeatureDocumentationView.tsx`, `GenerateFeatureDocButton.tsx`, `app/codeboard/features/[id]/documentation/page.tsx`, `markdownPresets.tsx`, `backend/api/doc_settings.py`, `backend/services/doc_settings_service.py`, `backend/app/main.py` (retention loop + autoGenerate gate), `backend/models/doc_settings.py`, `backend/models/schemas.py` DocSettings section.

**Verdict**: No CRITICAL or HIGH findings. Posture is consistent with the documented unauthenticated-LAN trust model. A handful of MEDIUM/LOW/INFO observations follow.

---

## CRITICAL
None.

## HIGH
None.

## MEDIUM

**M1 — Per-issue retention cap uses `IN (...)` set with no upper bound on members**
File: `backend/services/doc_settings_service.py:76-89`
The "survivors" select returns up to `maxPerIssue` rows (≤1000). Those IDs are then passed to `notin_(keep_ids)`. SQLAlchemy expands the set into a SQLite `NOT IN (?, ?, …)` parameter list. SQLite's default `SQLITE_MAX_VARIABLE_NUMBER` is 999 on older builds and 32766 on 3.32+. With `maxPerIssue=1000` you can hit the 999-variable ceiling on legacy SQLite and the DELETE will throw `sqlite3.OperationalError: too many SQL variables`. Effect: retention pass aborts that issue and the loop swallows it via the outer `except` in `process_doc_retention`, so the cap silently fails to apply. Not a security boundary, but a DoS-via-disk-exhaustion enabler over time. Recommend chunking the `notin_` set or inverting the delete (`executedAt &lt; oldest_keep_executedAt`).

**M2 — `apply_retention` walks all distinct issueIds in one transaction with no batching**
File: `backend/services/doc_settings_service.py:70-90`
With N issues × 2 queries each, a long-running tx on SQLite holds a write lock blocking the whole API. Hostile or accidental scale (50k issues) freezes the backend for the duration. Recommend periodic commit-per-batch or yield to event loop. Not exploitable cross-tenant since there are no tenants, but still a DoS surface.

## LOW

**L1 — `autoGenerate` defaults to ON when DocSettings load fails**
File: `backend/app/main.py:108-114`
The comment justifies fail-open; the practical risk is benign (extra ExecutionSummary rows, not data exposure). However, "DB tamper to drop the row" is harmless because the next call to `get_or_create_settings` re-creates the default row anyway. Conclusion: fail-mode is correct given the model. INFO-grade, but flagged since you asked.

**L2 — `JSON.parse(techStack)` with String-only filter — no prototype-pollution path**
File: `frontend/components/codeboard/FeatureDocumentationView.tsx:50-60`
`JSON.parse` is wrapped in try/catch; result is type-guarded as `Array&lt;string&gt;` and de-duped. Prototype pollution via `__proto__` in JSON is not exploitable here because (a) the parsed value is never spread/merged into another object, (b) only string elements survive the filter, (c) `Set` deduplication does not walk prototype chain. Safe.

**L3 — Markdown rendering surface verified clean**
Files: `FeatureDocumentationView.tsx:122-130`, `markdownPresets.tsx:32-52`
Confirmed: `urlTransform={defaultUrlTransform}` blocks `javascript:` / `vbscript:` / `data:` for both links and images. No `rehype-raw`, no `remark-gfm`, no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` anywhere in the three files. Anchor override forces `target=_blank rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow"`. Image override is not customised, so images inherit ReactMarkdown's default `&lt;img&gt;` — `urlTransform` still applies, so `src=javascript:` is stripped. Acceptable.

**L4 — Retention loop CancelledError handling is correct**
File: `backend/app/main.py:204-222`
`asyncio.CancelledError` is re-raised before the catch-all `except Exception`, so cooperative shutdown via `retention_task.cancel()` + `await retention_task` propagates cleanly. The lifespan's `try/except CancelledError: pass` after `await` matches. Correct.

**L5 — DocSettings PATCH bounds enforced by Pydantic**
File: `backend/models/schemas.py:725-730`
`retentionDays: ge=1, le=1825` and `maxPerIssue: ge=1, le=1000` block negative-overflow / DoS. `model_dump(exclude_unset=True)` + explicit `setattr(row, field, value)` only over keys present in the validated schema means arbitrary-field injection is impossible (Pydantic drops unknown fields by default in v2). Confirmed safe.

**L6 — `DocSettings.key` singleton is not enforced server-side beyond the `where` clause**
File: `backend/services/doc_settings_service.py:33` + `models/doc_settings.py:28`
Primary key is `key` and only `SINGLETON_KEY="global"` is ever queried/written. A direct DB write injecting another row is harmless because the service always selects `key == "global"`. Acceptable.

## INFO

**I1** — `formatIndexedAt` (`FeatureDocumentationView.tsx:66`) accepts `new Date(iso)` server input. NaN-guarded. No XSS or DoS path (output is text only).

**I2** — `GenerateFeatureDocButton.tsx:48-53` surfaces `err.message` directly to the user via toast. Backend errors should already be sanitised by `app/errors.py`; verify the doc-gen endpoint never leaks stack traces in `detail`. (Out of scope here, but worth a spot-check.)

**I3** — `app/codeboard/features/[id]/documentation/page.tsx:82-99` correctly type-guards `issue.type === 'FEATURE'` before rendering. URL `id` is `encodeURIComponent`-escaped in all `Link` href constructions.

**I4** — No multi-tenant access control gap because the system is documented as single-tenant unauthenticated-LAN; cross-tenant data exposure is N/A.

---

## Summary

| Severity | Count |
|---|---|
| CRITICAL | 0 |
| HIGH | 0 |
| MEDIUM | 2 (both DoS/operational, not exploitable) |
| LOW | 6 |
| INFO | 4 |

**Recommendation**: Address M1 (chunk the `notin_` set or invert the delete predicate) before scaling beyond ~1000 ExecutionSummary rows per issue. Everything else is sound. E2 frontend markdown rendering is correctly hardened. E3 backend Pydantic bounds, parameterised SQL, and cancellation semantics all check out. Safe to mark CB-2069 + CB-2091 ready for QA.

**Files referenced (absolute paths)**:
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/frontend/components/codeboard/FeatureDocumentationView.tsx
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/frontend/components/codeboard/GenerateFeatureDocButton.tsx
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/frontend/components/codeboard/markdownPresets.tsx
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/frontend/app/codeboard/features/[id]/documentation/page.tsx
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/backend/api/doc_settings.py
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/backend/services/doc_settings_service.py
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/backend/app/main.py
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/backend/models/doc_settings.py
- /Volumes/Seagate/Claude/ProjectsManagerWebV2Production/backend/models/schemas.py</result>

## Task Description
**Severity:** CRITICAL (defeats E1's user-visible deliverable)

**Location:** `frontend/components/service-monitor.tsx:208`

**Problem**

`RagStatusCard` calls `fetch('/api/system/rag/status')` with a relative URL. The frontend runs on Next.js at `:3601` and the FastAPI status endpoint lives at `:8401`. Every other `fetch('/api/...')` call in service-monitor.tsx has a corresponding Next.js route handler at `frontend/app/api/<path>/route.ts` that proxies to backend (`/api/projects/status`, `/api/docker/status`, `/api/watchdog/events`).

There is **no** `frontend/app/api/system/` directory and **no** rewrite in `frontend/next.config.ts` that forwards unknown `/api/*` paths to FastAPI. Verified:
- `find frontend/app/api -type d` → no `system/`
- `frontend/next.config.ts` → no `rewrites()`

Consequences:
- Browser fetch returns 404 every poll
- `failCountRef` reaches threshold after the second poll
- Badge permanently red: "RAG offline — status endpoint unreachable"
- The very surface E1 was supposed to add is silently broken

**Fix (pick one)**

Option A (recommended, mirrors existing pattern): add `frontend/app/api/system/rag/status/route.ts` that proxies to `http://localhost:8401/api/system/rag/status`, mirroring the shape of `frontend/app/api/projects/status/route.ts`.

Option B: change the fetch to the absolute backend origin (`http://localhost:8401/...`). CSP `connect-src` already permits `http://localhost:*`. Less consistent with rest of file.

**Required regression test**

Smoke test must hit through the Next.js layer (browser/3601), not just FastAPI (curl/8401). The original E1 backend tests pass while the user-facing card stays broken.

**Found in:** code-reviewer pass on CB-2048.


Please implement this task. When you're done, summarize what you did.

## Sources Consulted

1. **Building Your Agent Team** (id: cmnudfue5003rmy0zlxt7ioa1) — 15 pages, read in full. Primary source for orchestration architecture, inter-agent communication, memory systems, and UI visibility patterns.
2. **Building Effective Agents** (id: cmljg1ebo000pmn0zgxkxg3vk) — 17 pages, read in full. Source for workflow/agent taxonomy, parallelization patterns, tool format design (ACI), and the core simplicity principle.
3. **Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents** (id: cmljgr6n5004nmn0zovcu47ca) — 1 page, read in full. Source for context rot, compaction, structured note-taking, and sub-agent context isolation patterns.
4. **Writing Effective Tools for Agents** (id: cmljgr20k0043mn0zj85ycyu3) — 1 page, read in full. Source for tool design principles, namespacing, response design, token efficiency, and description engineering.
5. **Stripe Minions** (id: cmo4d0v8n04eiqp0zm34yjhyr) — 4 pages, read in full. Source for production-scale unattended agents, Blueprint orchestration, Toolshed MCP, shift-feedback-left, and "what's good for humans is good for agents."
6. **How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code** (id: cmlp87a2800btmf0zlsdf0drn) — 10 pages, read in full. Source for production team patterns, async/sync task classification, sub-agent specialization, and parallel session management.

---

## Core Findings

### 1. Orchestrating Multiple Persistent Agent Sessions Per Project Tab

**The orchestrator-workers pattern is the right shape for Feature Studio.** Jonny is the orchestrator; skills/agents are workers with dedicated context windows. The key distinction from simple parallelization: "subtasks are determined dynamically by the orchestrator based on the specific input" rather than pre-defined (*Building Effective Agents*, p. 10).

**Template vs. instance separation is how you support persistence.** Each agent/skill gets a `template/` (git-tracked, versioned: identity, capabilities, domain knowledge) and a separate `instance/` (gitignored: accumulated memory, session state, logs). "If you delete an instance, the agent loses its learned knowledge but retains its identity and capabilities." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 6). Applied to Feature Studio: Jonny and each skill have separate template definitions, but share a project-scoped `COMPANY.md` that is the single source of truth for the feature context and team roster.

**The GroupQueue pattern prevents session corruption.** One execution at a time per agent, enforced by a folder lock that prevents the same agent from executing across two tabs simultaneously. Without this, "two processes reading the same session, both appending responses, interleaving their writes" corrupts conversation history (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 6). For Feature Studio, each skill's session must be serialized even when multiple browser tabs invoke the same project.

**From Anthropic teams in production:** "When working on long-running data tasks, they open multiple instances of Claude Code in different repositories for different projects. Each instance maintains full context, so when they switch back after hours or days, Claude Code remembers exactly what they were doing." (*How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code*, p. 1). This validates the multi-tab, per-project-tab persistence model — it is already how power users operate.

**Stripe's pre-warmed devboxes** are the production equivalent: isolated, clean environments spun up in 10 seconds with "Stripe code and services pre-loaded." The key property: "parallelism, predictability, and isolation." "What's good for humans is good for agents." (*Stripe Minions*, Part 2). For Feature Studio, each project session is effectively a logical devbox: isolated context, pre-loaded project state (CLAUDE.md, CodeBoard issues), clean working memory per run.

---

### 2. Surfacing Live Agent and Skill Activity in UI Without Leaking Internal State

**The Visibility Principle is the most important design constraint.** "All agent communication must be visible and auditable. If you cannot see a message in your team's communication platform, it did not happen." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 5, Ch. 3). The author learned this after an agent claimed to have communicated through an "internal channel" that did not exist — it hallucinated the interaction. The lesson: if the platform doesn't surface it, it didn't happen, and you cannot debug it.

**What to surface vs. what to hide:** The right model is showing the what (which skill is active, which tool was called, what was produced) without flooding the user with the how (raw prompt content, intermediate reasoning dumps, full context state). From Stripe: "engineers can see the decisions and actions the minion took in a web UI" (*Stripe Minions*, Part 1) — this is a post-hoc audit view, not a live stream of internal state. The activity panel should show: which skill was invoked, its current phase (thinking / calling tool / producing output), and completed artifacts.

**Explicit agent identity is critical in shared views.** When multiple agents share a channel, "visual distinctiveness is the difference between a coherent team and a confusing wall of text." In Discord, each agent gets its own webhook identity — distinct name, distinct avatar. "You can immediately see which agent responded, which agent is addressing which message, and who is talking to whom." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 7). In Feature Studio's live activity panel, each skill card must have a stable visual identity (name, color, icon) distinct from Jonny's orchestrator stream.

**Passive-by-default prevents noise.** "Agents respond only when explicitly tagged. They hear everything in their channel, but they stay quiet until someone says @keeper or @herald. This eliminates 90% of the chaos." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). In the UI: skill agents should not generate visual output until Jonny explicitly dispatches them. The activity panel shows "waiting" vs "active" states, not a constant stream of internal chatter.

**The "slot machine" anti-pattern warning.** Anthropic's data science team describes: "commit their state, let Claude work autonomously for 30 minutes, and either accept the solution or restart fresh if it doesn't work." (*How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code*, p. 5). The implication for UI: show progress in phases (dispatched → thinking → producing → done), with a clear restart/abort path. Don't give users a stream of tokens that creates the false impression the agent can be steered mid-execution.

---

### 3. Inter-Agent Communication for Collaborative Feature Planning

**Four typed message primitives.** The system that works in production uses exactly four inter-agent message types with different semantics and permission profiles:
- `notify` — one-directional information share, no reply expected
- `request` — question requiring an answer (blocks until resolved)
- `delegate` — task handoff, sender is done with it
- `broadcast` — notify a group simultaneously

"These types are not just labels. They drive policy enforcement." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). Applied to Feature Studio: Jonny dispatches skills via `delegate`. Skills can `notify` Jonny when complete. Skills should not be able to `request` from each other unless explicitly designed for cross-skill collaboration (e.g., the UX skill requesting the product context from the research skill).

**The 13-step delivery flow is the audit trail.** Every inter-agent message goes through: identity validation → policy check → rate limit → cooldown → chain depth → target resolution → counter update → format → send (visibly) → store in DB → enqueue → log to audit table (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). For Feature Studio, every Jonny-to-skill and skill-to-Jonny message must hit the database with a correlation ID. This is what makes the Feature Backlog "add to CodeBoard" action reliable — the trail proves the planning conversation actually produced the artifact.

**Chain depth limiting prevents runaway delegation.** `max_chain_depth: 3` means if Jonny notifies Skill A, which notifies Skill B, which tries to notify someone else, the chain stops. "This prevents infinite relay chains where agents keep passing work to each other without anyone actually doing anything." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). For Feature Studio, the chain should be at most: User → Jonny → Skill → (optional sub-skill). Enforce this structurally.

**COMPANY.md / shared context is domain separation without code.** Every agent loads a shared roster table that defines who handles what. "When every agent knows every other agent's domain, they naturally defer rather than stepping on each other's toes." The rule encoded: "When something belongs to another agent's domain, say so and defer." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). For Feature Studio: Jonny's system prompt + each skill's system prompt must all load a shared "Feature Studio context" block that defines each skill's scope. Skills do not improvise outside their domain.

**The hallucination risk is highest in inter-agent communication.** The author's "agent shaming story" — an agent said "I've notified Herald" but there was no notification — demonstrates that agents will describe completed actions they never took. "The agent fabricated the communication. It did what LLMs do when they cannot actually perform an action: it described the action as completed and moved on." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 5). The fix: every inter-agent action must go through a real API call that produces a visible record. Jonny's claim that "UX skill has been dispatched" must be backed by a database record, not just Jonny's assertion.

---

### 4. Tool Design for "Add to CodeBoard" / "Send to AutoPilot"

**Consolidate, don't decompose.** "Multiple API calls can be wrapped into single, higher-level operations. Rather than forcing an agent to orchestrate a chain of low-level calls, provide a single tool that handles the orchestration internally." (*Writing Effective Tools for Agents*, p. 1).

Anti-pattern: giving Jonny separate `create_epic`, `create_story`, `create_task`, `set_parent`, `set_assignee` tools.
Pattern: one `add_to_codeboard(feature_id, hierarchy)` tool that handles the full issue tree internally.

Similarly: `send_to_autopilot(issue_id, priority)` is a single action — don't make Jonny call `update_status` + `enqueue_task` + `set_assignee` separately.

**Search over listing.** "list_contacts: Returns all contacts, potentially hundreds or thousands of entries. Consumes enormous context and forces the agent to filter results. search_contacts: Takes a query parameter and returns only relevant results." (*Writing Effective Tools for Agents*, p. 1). For Feature Studio: `search_codeboard_issues(query, project_id)` beats `list_all_issues(project_id)`. Jonny should be able to find existing issues to link against without loading the full board into context.

**Semantic responses, not technical metadata.** "Prioritize semantic meaning over technical metadata in response fields: prefer `name` over `uuid`, `in_progress` over status code `3`." (*Writing Effective Tools for Agents*, p. 1). When `add_to_codeboard` returns, it should confirm: `{ epic: "CB-2060: Feature Studio UI", stories: ["CB-2061", "CB-2062"], status: "created" }` — not raw IDs only.

**Actionable errors guide the agent.** "Good error response: 'Results truncated to 50 items. Use the query parameter to narrow your search, or use offset to paginate.' Poor error response: 'Error code: 413.'" (*Writing Effective Tools for Agents*, p. 1). When `send_to_autopilot` fails because the queue is paused, the tool should return: `"AutoPilot queue is currently paused (token_exhaustion). Issue has been staged. Resume via /api/execute/queue/resume when reset time passes."` — not a 503.

**Poka-yoke (mistake-proofing) in tool parameters.** "Change the arguments so that it is harder to make mistakes... rather than adding instructions to 'remember to use absolute paths,' the tool was redesigned so that relative paths were simply not an option." (*Building Effective Agents*, p. 16). Applied: `add_to_codeboard` should require `project_id` explicitly (never infer from context), and `issue_type` should be an enum (FEATURE / EPIC / STORY / TASK), not a free-text field.

**Description engineering is prompting.** "Think of tool descriptions as prompts... A well-written description answers: When should the agent use this tool? What does it need to provide? What will it get back? What should it do with the results?" (*Writing Effective Tools for Agents*, p. 1). Jonny's tool descriptions for CodeBoard and AutoPilot tools should explicitly state the trigger condition ("use this when the user says 'add to backlog' or 'create issues for this'") and the expected post-call behavior ("after calling, confirm the issue keys to the user").

**Toolshed pattern for shared capability.** Stripe built "a centralized internal MCP server called Toolshed, which makes it easy for Stripe engineers to author new tools and make them automatically discoverable to our agentic systems... Adding a tool to Toolshed immediately grants capabilities to our whole fleet of hundreds of different agents." (*Stripe Minions*, Part 2). For Feature Studio: CodeBoard operations should live in the existing MCP/tool layer and be available to all skills, not duplicated per skill. Each skill gets a curated subset.

---

### 5. Context Engineering for Multi-Session Token Efficiency

**Context rot is real and measurable.** "As the number of tokens in the context window increases, the model's ability to accurately recall information from that context decreases... Simply stuffing more information into the context window does not reliably improve performance — it can actively degrade it." (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). For Feature Studio: each skill's session must be kept tight. Don't pass Jonny's full planning history to every skill — pass only what that skill needs for its specific task.

**Sub-agents provide clean context isolation.** "Specialized sub-agents handle focused tasks with clean, dedicated context windows while a main orchestrating agent coordinates the overall effort. Each sub-agent can explore extensively within its domain but returns a condensed, distilled summary of its work — often just 1,000 to 2,000 tokens." (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). This is the exact model for Feature Studio: Jonny's context stays clean (the planning conversation); each skill gets a fresh context window for its task; the skill returns a compact artifact (a feature breakdown, a UX spec snippet, a risk list) that Jonny ingests.

**Just-in-time context beats pre-loading everything.** "Rather than pre-processing and loading all potentially relevant data into the context upfront, effective agents maintain lightweight identifiers — file paths, stored queries, web links — and dynamically load detailed data via tools at runtime only when needed." (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). For Jonny: don't inject the full CodeBoard issue list at session start. Give Jonny a `search_codeboard` tool and let it pull issues when the user asks about them.

**Compaction for long planning conversations.** "Compaction involves taking a conversation nearing the context window limit, summarizing its contents, and reinitiating a new context window with the summary... The agent continues with the compressed context plus the five most recently accessed files." (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). Feature Studio planning sessions can run long. The system must detect approaching context limits and compact automatically — preserving the planning decisions and open questions, discarding the tool call transcripts.

**Structured note-taking across sessions.** "Rather than relying solely on compaction, agents can regularly write notes persisted to memory outside of the context window. These notes are pulled back into the context window at later times when needed." (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). Jonny should write a `PLANNING_STATE.md` after each significant planning step — decisions made, options still open, issues created. This is what survives a session restart and what child skills inherit as their briefing.

**The system prompt assembly order matters.** From the production codebase: identity (AGENT.md) first, shared context (COMPANY.md) second, tools third, memory last. "Content earlier in the system prompt has stronger influence on agent behavior. That is why identity comes first and memory comes last. The agent's core personality should not be overridden by an accumulated memory entry." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 6). For Jonny: his role as "collaborative planning lead" must come before any project-specific context. Don't let a specific project's history override Jonny's core behavior.

**The right altitude for system prompts.** "The optimal system prompt is specific enough to guide behavior effectively, yet flexible enough to provide the model with strong heuristics for handling situations not explicitly covered." Avoid both extremes: brittle over-specification and vague underspecification. (*Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents*, p. 1). Jonny's system prompt should define his role and decision framework, not try to enumerate every possible user request.

---

### 6. Production Lessons on UX: What Worked vs. Failed

**Worked: Slack/chat as primary UX, not a dashboard.** Stripe: "A typical minion run starts in a Slack message and ends in a pull request." (*Stripe Minions*, Part 1). The author of Building Your Agent Team: "The best interface for managing AI agents turned out to be my phone. I can tag an agent while commuting, ask it to draft something, and review the result when I arrive." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 7). Feature Studio's chat-first model is validated. The Feature Backlog board is the secondary surface (artifact review), not the primary interaction.

**Worked: Asynchronous by default, synchronous when needed.** Anthropic product team: "develop task classification intuition — learn to distinguish between tasks that work well asynchronously (peripheral features, prototyping) versus those needing synchronous supervision (core business logic, critical fixes)." (*How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code*, p. 3). Feature Studio must support both modes: fire a skill and come back later (async), or watch it work step by step (sync/supervised). A single UX that forces one or the other will frustrate both use cases.

**Worked: Start with human approval, loosen as trust builds.** "Start with human approval, observe quality, then gradually expand autonomy. Never go straight to full autonomy on day one. You need to build trust with your own agents, just like you would with a new hire." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 4). Applied to "send to AutoPilot": the first time Jonny proposes sending a set of issues to AutoPilot, show the user the proposed queue and ask for confirmation. After consistent quality, allow one-click dispatch with a confirmation summary.

**Worked: Blueprints — hybrid deterministic + agentic flow.** "Blueprints combine the determinism of workflows with agents' flexibility in dealing with the unknown: a given node can run either deterministic code or an agent loop focused on a task." (*Stripe Minions*, Part 2). Writing linters and git operations were deterministic nodes; "Implement task" was agentic. The Feature Studio parallel skill execution should follow this: the dispatch of skills, collection of artifacts, and CodeBoard push are deterministic steps; what each skill does within its scope is agentic.

**Failed: Proactive agents without triggers.** "Early in development, I made agents proactive. If Seneschal heard me mention a meeting in another channel, it would immediately jump in. In practice, it was noise. Constant, relentless noise from agents trying to be useful without being asked." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). Anti-pattern for Feature Studio: skills should not auto-trigger on keywords in the chat. They must be explicitly dispatched by Jonny (orchestrator), not by keyword detection. The UX implication: the user talks to Jonny; Jonny decides when to use skills; skills never self-activate.

**Failed: Hidden/internal agent communication.** The "agent shaming story" — an agent told the user it had notified another agent through an "internal channel" that didn't exist. Hallucinated coordination. The fix was forcing all communication through a visible medium. (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 5). Anti-pattern for Feature Studio: never rely on Jonny's assertion that a skill was invoked. The backend must record every skill dispatch as a DB record before treating it as real.

**Failed: Framework over-engineering.** "I spent more time fighting the framework than building agents... The framework was approximately 434,000 lines of code. That is not a typo." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 5). The Anthropic guidance echoes: "Do not hesitate to reduce abstraction layers and build with basic components as you move to production." (*Building Effective Agents*, p. 17). For Feature Studio: keep the orchestration layer thin. The Jonny-to-skill dispatch is a few hundred lines of well-structured code, not a framework.

**Failed: Two-agent parallelism without domain separation.** Without a shared roster table defining each agent's domain, agents step on each other. The working solution: every agent loads the same COMPANY.md with an explicit domain table and the rule "defer, don't improvise." (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 10). For Feature Studio: when Jonny and a product skill are both active on the same project, their domain boundaries must be explicit in both their system prompts. Jonny orchestrates and synthesizes. The skill does the domain-specific analysis. They do not overlap.

**Anthropic's two-step planning pattern.** Multiple Anthropic teams independently converged on: "Plan in Claude.ai first, build in Claude Code." (*How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code*, p. 10). The growth marketing team: "Spend significant time upfront using Claude.ai to think through your entire workflow, then have Claude.ai create a comprehensive prompt and code structure for Claude Code to reference." This validates Feature Studio's chat-first planning loop — users talk, Jonny synthesizes, only then do skills execute or artifacts get pushed to CodeBoard.

**Stripe's shift-feedback-left principle applies to planning artifacts too.** "If we know an automated check will fail CI, it's best if it's also enforced in the IDE and presented to the engineer right away." (*Stripe Minions*, Part 2). For Feature Studio: validate proposed CodeBoard issue hierarchies (parent linkage, required fields, assignee validity) in the chat surface before pushing to CodeBoard — not after. Show the proposed issue tree to the user for review before the `add_to_codeboard` tool fires.

---

## Additional Insights

**Model tiering for cost efficiency.** The production pattern is: Haiku for extraction/summarization (post-conversation memory), Sonnet for agent conversations, Opus reserved for direct human sessions. (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 11). Feature Studio should tier accordingly: lightweight skills (summarize, categorize) can run on Sonnet; Jonny as orchestrator and any skill doing deep analysis uses Sonnet or better.

**Session separation for quality-critical outputs.** "Write the feature in one session. Open a NEW session. Tell it to write tests. Open a THIRD session to make the tests pass." The principle: adversarial tension between sessions produces higher-quality outputs than a single session doing everything. (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 12). Applied: when Feature Studio generates a feature breakdown, having a second pass (a validation skill) review the breakdown before it hits CodeBoard catches gaps the first skill wouldn't catch in its own output.

**Approval modes are per-agent and per-action, not global.** `confidence` mode (agent decides based on its own confidence), `always` mode (human approves everything), `never` mode (full autonomy). Marketing/publishing agents always run in `always` mode; product/analytical agents can run in `confidence` mode. (*Building Your Agent Team*, p. 13). For "send to AutoPilot": the first few runs should be `always` mode. For "add to CodeBoard": `confidence` mode is appropriate since the action is reversible.

---

## Citation Summary

**Books read:** Building Your Agent Team: A Practitioner's Guide to Multi-Agent AI Systems · Building Effective Agents · Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents · Writing Effective Tools for Agents · Stripe Minions — Unattended Coding Agents at Scale (Parts 1+2) · How Anthropic Teams Use Claude Code

**Key takeaways:** Template/instance separation is how you version agent behavior independently from accumulated state · All inter-agent communication must be visible and auditable — never trust an agent's claim that it communicated without a DB record · Context rot is real: sub-agents with clean dedicated context windows outperform single-agent approaches on complex parallel tasks · Tool consolidation (one high-level tool per workflow) dramatically reduces agent errors versus many granular tools · Proactive agents without explicit triggers create noise — passive by default, respond only when dispatched · Blueprints (hybrid deterministic + agentic state machines) outperform pure agent loops at production scale · "What's good for humans is good for agents" — invest in the same isolation, feedback loops, and tooling you'd build for human engineers

**Impact:** These books provide production-validated patterns (Stripe at 1,300+ PRs/week; Anthropic across 10 internal teams) that go beyond theoretical architecture — specifically the agent shaming anti-pattern, the COMPANY.md domain separation technique, and the session separation for quality that are not covered in standard multi-agent literature.

**Worth remembering:** "I caught an agent once claiming it had communicated with another agent through an 'internal channel' that did not exist. It hallucinated the entire interaction and presented fabricated results. Since then, the rule is absolute: every message, every agent-to-agent notification, every response goes through Discord where I can see it, scroll back through it, and audit it." — *Building Your Agent Team*, pp. 5 &amp; 10. This is the single most practically important lesson for Feature Studio: any coordination Jonny claims to have done with a skill that isn't backed by a real database record is a hallucination dressed as infrastructure.</result>

## Sources Consulted

1. **UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents** (id: cmmfdl2z503qep10zhi9dp1m4) — Chapters 3–7 (visual hierarchy, typography, color, spacing, components) and Chapters 10–12 (behavioral triggers, microinteractions, animation, persuasion, form design). This is the deepest rulebook source.

## Core Findings

---

### 1. Chat-First Interfaces with Multiple Conversation Tabs

**Tab navigation pattern (Linear-inspired):**
Linear's tab system uses Inter Variable 13–14px at weight 510 on a `#0f1011` panel background. Active tabs lighten to `#f7f8f8`; inactive stay at `#d0d6e0`. Tab containers get `border-bottom: 1px solid rgba(255,255,255,0.05)` — never a solid color separator. Tab pills use `border-radius: 9999px` for filter-style vs `border-radius: 6px` for functional tabs.

For a chat planning UI with tabs, the architectural recommendation from Vercel's composition guidelines is the **Compound Component + Context pattern**:

&gt; "Avoid boolean prop proliferation… use composition: `&lt;Composer.Frame&gt;`, `&lt;Composer.Input&gt;`, `&lt;Composer.Footer&gt;`, `&lt;Composer.Submit&gt;`" — *Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines*, Page 3

Each conversation tab should be a `ThreadComposer` variant wrapping shared `Composer.Provider` state — the provider holds `{ state, actions, meta }` and children subscribe via `use(ComposerContext)`. This decouples tab state management from rendering.

**Tab visual recipe (shadcn/ui-aligned, dark-mode-native):**
```tsx
// Tab bar
className="flex border-b border-white/5 bg-[#0f1011]"

// Active tab
className="px-4 py-2 text-[13px] font-[510] text-[#f7f8f8] border-b-2 border-[#7170ff]"

// Inactive tab
className="px-4 py-2 text-[13px] font-[510] text-[#d0d6e0] hover:text-[#f7f8f8] transition-colors"
```

The indigo-violet underline `#7170ff` is Linear's only chromatic accent — appropriate for "selected" state. Everything else stays achromatic.

**Anthropic (Claude) approach for light-mode chat:**
Claude's design uses Parchment `#f5f4ed` as base surface. Conversation tabs would use Anthropic Serif at 20px weight 500 for tab headings, Anthropic Sans at 15–16px for body content. Active tab indicator: Terracotta `#c96442` as bottom border. The serif typeface in tab headers signals "each conversation is a document" rather than a technical task.

&gt; "Serif for authority, sans for utility: Anthropic Serif carries all headline content with medium weight (500)…Anthropic Sans handles all functional UI text — buttons, labels, navigation — with quiet efficiency." — *Awesome Design.md*, Page 7

---

### 2. Split-Pane Layouts with Artifact Preview (Right-Pane Reveal Animation)

**Animation timing rules (from UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 10 + Vercel Ch. 16):**

The split-pane reveal when an artifact appears follows the **Asymmetric Exit / Enter Animation rule**:

- Enter (pane slides in): `250ms, translateX(100% → 0), opacity 0 → 1, ease-out`
- Exit (pane slides out when dismissed): `150ms, translateX(0 → 4-8%), opacity 1 → 0` — ~60% of enter duration

&gt; "Exits should get out of the way. Keep the motion in the same direction family as the enter… shorten duration and reduce travel distance." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 10

Use CSS `transition`, not `@keyframes`, for the pane open/close state because transitions are **interruptible** — if the user dismisses mid-open, the animation picks up from current position without a jarring reset:

```css
.artifact-pane {
  transition: transform 250ms ease-out, opacity 250ms ease-out;
}
.artifact-pane[data-state="closed"] {
  transform: translateX(100%);
  opacity: 0;
}
.artifact-pane[data-state="open"] {
  transform: translateX(0);
  opacity: 1;
}
```

**GPU-only properties (from Vercel Ch. 16):**
```css
/* Good — GPU accelerated */
.artifact-pane {
  transform: translateX(100px);
  transition: transform 200ms ease-out;
}
```
Never animate `width`, `left`, or `margin` for split pane transitions — these trigger layout, causing jank.

**Content-visibility for chat message list (Vercel Ch. 6):**
```css
.message-item {
  content-visibility: auto;
  contain-intrinsic-size: 0 80px;
}
```
This virtualizes off-screen messages without a virtual scroll library — essential if the pane split reduces visible chat area.

**Stagger animation for artifact content loading:**
When the right pane opens and content renders in sections (code block → explanation → actions), use 50–100ms stagger between chunks:

&gt; "Stagger each by ~50-100ms. The eye tracks motion sequentially, so a staggered entry reads as 'this is a composition of items'… cap cascade length at ~400ms." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 10

---

### 3. Live Agent Activity Panels with Non-Distracting Animation

**The key principle from UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 10:**

&gt; "Feedback should follow three principles: (1) Minimal — the least amount necessary to communicate the state change. Don't animate what can be shown statically. (2) Non-arbitrary — every feedback element must have a reason tied to the interaction's meaning. (3) Personality-bearing — feedback is where product personality lives." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 10

**Agent status badge recipe (Linear-inspired):**

```tsx
// Idle badge
&lt;span className="inline-flex items-center gap-1 rounded-full 
  px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] font-[510] 
  bg-transparent border border-[#23252a] text-[#8a8f98]"&gt;
  Idle
&lt;/span&gt;

// Active badge — green dot pulses
&lt;span className="inline-flex items-center gap-1.5 rounded-full 
  px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] font-[510] 
  bg-transparent border border-[#23252a] text-[#f7f8f8]"&gt;
  &lt;span className="h-1.5 w-1.5 rounded-full bg-[#27a644] animate-pulse" /&gt;
  Running
&lt;/span&gt;
```

The green status dot `#27a644` / `#10b981` is Linear's only success color, used strictly for status indicators. The `animate-pulse` CSS keyframe is appropriate here because it's a **one-shot looping sequence** (agent continuously active), not an interactive state change — keyframes are correct per Vercel's interruptible-motion rule.

**Streaming tool status (from Claude Code SDK streaming docs, p. 336–338):**
The official SDK pattern for showing "agent is working" in a UI uses an `inTool` boolean flag that drives the activity indicator:

```typescript
// From Claude Code Official Docs, Page 336 — Build a streaming UI
let inTool = false;
for await (const message of query({ options: { includePartialMessages: true }})) {
  if (message.type === "stream_event") {
    const event = message.event;
    if (event.type === "content_block_start" &amp;&amp; event.content_block.type === "tool_use") {
      // Show [Using ToolName...] indicator
      inTool = true;
    } else if (event.type === "content_block_stop" &amp;&amp; inTool) {
      // Show "done", hide indicator  
      inTool = false;
    } else if (event.type === "content_block_delta" &amp;&amp; event.delta.type === "text_delta" &amp;&amp; !inTool) {
      // Stream text only outside tool calls
    }
  }
}
```

&gt; "This pattern is useful for chat interfaces that need to show progress during multi-step agent tasks." — *Claude Code: Official Docs*, Page 336

**Timing rule for live activity panels:**
Feedback timing under 100ms for direct manipulation states. Agent activity panels are system triggers (not user-initiated), so they use `aria-live="polite"` — announced after the current speech finishes, never interrupting.

**Reduced motion compliance (Vercel Ch. 16):**
```css
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .animate-pulse { animation-duration: 0.01ms !important; }
}
```
Replace the pulse animation with a static `opacity: 0.7` for the green dot when reduced motion is preferred.

---

### 4. Feature Backlog Boards with Priority and Scheduling Controls

**Linear's issue board design principles:**

Linear's board is built on `#08090a` (deepest dark) canvas with panels at `#0f1011`. Cards use `rgba(255,255,255,0.02)` background with `1px solid rgba(255,255,255,0.08)` border — **never solid backgrounds**, always translucent. Card radius: 8px standard, 12px for featured/prioritized items.

**Priority badge recipe:**
```tsx
// Critical priority
&lt;span className="inline-flex items-center gap-1 rounded-[2px] 
  px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] font-[510] uppercase tracking-wide
  bg-red-500/10 text-red-400 border border-red-500/20"&gt;
  Critical
&lt;/span&gt;

// Medium priority  
&lt;span className="rounded-[2px] px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] font-[510]
  bg-white/5 text-[#d0d6e0] border border-white/5"&gt;
  Medium
&lt;/span&gt;
```

Key rule: `border-radius: 2px` (Micro) for inline badges on a Linear-style board. `border-radius: 9999px` only for filter pills, not priority tags.

**Scheduling controls — Doherty Threshold:**
&gt; "The Doherty Threshold: maintain system response below 400ms to keep users in flow state. Users who must wait more than 400ms context-switch mentally. Below 400ms they stay engaged." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 9

Schedule selectors and drag-to-reorder must respond within 400ms. For date/priority dropdowns, use `transition-duration: 150ms` on open (fast enough to feel instant, long enough to feel smooth).

**Information hierarchy for the board (from UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 3):**
The book codifies the "Propositional Density" rule — each card should carry only: key (CB-XXX), title, status badge, assignee avatar, priority tag. Everything else is secondary and revealed on hover or in a detail panel. This is the Linear pattern exactly: dense information in a scannable format with a deep-link detail view.

**Stripe's approach for scheduling controls (financial-precision aesthetic):**
If priority/scheduling is time-sensitive (e.g., deadline tracking), use Stripe's typographic choice for numerical data: `font-feature-settings: "tnum"` (tabular numerals) on countdown timers and date displays. This keeps numbers optically aligned across rows:

&gt; "Two OpenType modes: 'ss01' for display/body text, 'tnum' for tabular numerals in financial data." — *Awesome Design.md*, Page 47

---

### 5. Next.js 16 / Turbopack Streaming Patterns for SSE and stream-json

**From Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines — eliminating waterfalls:**

The #1 performance killer for a streaming chat UI is sequential awaits. The critical pattern:

```typescript
// WRONG: sequential, blocks both branches
const session = await auth();
const config = await fetchConfig();

// CORRECT: parallel start  
const sessionPromise = auth();
const configPromise = fetchConfig();
const session = await sessionPromise;
const [config, data] = await Promise.all([configPromise, fetchData(session.user.id)]);
```

&gt; "Waterfalls are the #1 performance killer. Each sequential await adds full network latency." — *Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines*, Page 2

**Suspense boundaries for chat panes:**
```tsx
function ChatPage() {
  return (
    &lt;div className="flex h-screen"&gt;
      &lt;ChatPane /&gt;  {/* streams independently */}
      &lt;Suspense fallback={&lt;ArtifactPaneSkeleton /&gt;}&gt;
        &lt;ArtifactPane /&gt;  {/* suspends while artifact loads */}
      &lt;/Suspense&gt;
    &lt;/div&gt;
  );
}
```

**Streaming from Claude Agent SDK (from Claude Code Official Docs, pp. 335–338):**

The `include_partial_messages: true` option enables `StreamEvent` objects containing raw SSE events. The message flow:
```
StreamEvent (message_start)
StreamEvent (content_block_start) — text block
StreamEvent (content_block_delta) — text chunks arriving
StreamEvent (content_block_stop)
AssistantMessage — complete, arrives last
ResultMessage — final
```

&gt; "By default, the Agent SDK yields complete AssistantMessage objects after Claude finishes generating each response. To receive incremental updates as text and tool calls are generated, enable partial message streaming." — *Claude Code: Official Docs*, Page 336

**Streaming with `after()` for non-blocking analytics (Vercel Ch. 3):**
```tsx
import { after } from 'next/server';
export async function POST(request: Request) {
  const result = await handleChatMessage(request);
  after(async () =&gt; {
    logChatEvent({ sessionId: result.sessionId }); // runs after response sent
  });
  return Response.json(result);
}
```

**Per-request deduplication for multi-tab chat:**
```typescript
import { cache } from 'react';
export const getCurrentSession = cache(async (sessionId: string) =&gt; {
  return await db.session.findUnique({ where: { id: sessionId } });
});
```
`React.cache()` deduplicates per render tree — multiple tabs querying the same session hit the DB only once.

---

### 6. shadcn/ui-Aligned Component Composition for Chat Tabs, Agent Badges, and Animation States

**Compound Component pattern (Vercel composition guidelines):**
```tsx
// Provider-based composition — the recommended pattern
const ChatContext = createContext(null);

const Chat = {
  Provider: ChatProvider,
  TabBar: ChatTabBar,
  Tab: ChatTab,
  MessageList: ChatMessageList,
  Input: ChatInput,
  AgentStatus: ChatAgentStatus,
};

// Usage:
&lt;Chat.Provider sessionId={sessionId} tabs={tabs}&gt;
  &lt;Chat.TabBar /&gt;
  &lt;Chat.MessageList /&gt;
  &lt;Chat.AgentStatus /&gt;
  &lt;Chat.Input /&gt;
&lt;/Chat.Provider&gt;
```

&gt; "Decouple state management from UI. Context holds { state, actions, meta }." — *Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines*, Page 3

**Icon swap animation for agent state changes (UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 10):**
When the agent transitions from Idle → Running → Done, cross-fade the icon:
```css
.agent-icon-enter {
  animation: icon-in 200ms ease-out forwards;
}
@keyframes icon-in {
  from { opacity: 0; transform: scale(0.25); filter: blur(4px); }
  to   { opacity: 1; transform: scale(1);    filter: blur(0);   }
}
```
&gt; "Spring bounce must be 0 at icon scale; any higher feels cartoonish on a 16-24px symbol." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 10

**shadcn/ui primitives alignment:**
Linear's entire component foundation is confirmed to be Radix UI primitives. This means shadcn/ui (which also uses Radix) produces pixel-level compatible behavior. The key shadcn components for this UI:
- `Tabs` (Radix TabsList/TabsTrigger/TabsContent) for conversation tabs
- `ScrollArea` for message list
- `Badge` for agent status and priority
- `Popover` for scheduling controls
- `Dialog` for artifact detail
- `ResizablePanelGroup` (via `react-resizable-panels`) for split pane

---

### 7. Accessibility Patterns for Live-Updating Regions and Split Panes

**`aria-live` regions for streaming text (from Vercel Ch. 13 + UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 14):**
```tsx
&lt;div role="status" aria-live="polite" aria-label="Agent response"&gt;
  {streamingText}
&lt;/div&gt;
```
Use `aria-live="polite"` for agent responses — waits for the user to finish their current interaction before announcing. Use `aria-live="assertive"` only for errors or critical interruptions.

&gt; "Semantic HTML first: `&lt;div role='status' aria-live='polite'&gt;{statusMessage}&lt;/div&gt;`" — *Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines*, Page 4

**Focus management for split-pane reveal:**
```tsx
const artifactPaneRef = useRef&lt;HTMLDivElement&gt;(null);
useEffect(() =&gt; {
  if (isArtifactVisible) {
    artifactPaneRef.current?.focus();
  }
}, [isArtifactVisible]);
```
When the artifact pane opens, focus must move into it. When it closes, focus returns to the triggering element (the message that produced the artifact).

**Visible focus indicators (Vercel Ch. 14):**
```css
button:focus-visible {
  outline: 2px solid #7170ff;  /* Linear's accent on dark */
  outline-offset: 2px;
}
/* OR for Vercel-style: */
button:focus-visible {
  outline: 2px solid hsla(212, 100%, 48%, 1);
  outline-offset: 2px;
}
```
The `focus-visible` pseudo-class shows outlines only for keyboard navigation — not on mouse click — reducing visual clutter for mouse users while maintaining full keyboard accessibility.

**Split pane keyboard navigation:**
The resize handle between chat and artifact panes must be keyboard-operable:
```tsx
&lt;div
  role="separator"
  aria-orientation="vertical"
  aria-valuenow={paneWidth}
  aria-valuemin={30}
  aria-valuemax={70}
  tabIndex={0}
  onKeyDown={(e) =&gt; {
    if (e.key === 'ArrowLeft') adjustPaneWidth(-5);
    if (e.key === 'ArrowRight') adjustPaneWidth(5);
  }}
/&gt;
```

**Content at 200% zoom (UI/UX Design Principles Ch. 14):**
&gt; "Content must be usable at 200% zoom… Never set `user-scalable=no` or `maximum-scale=1.0`." — *UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents*, Page 14

Split panes at 200% zoom should collapse to a single column (stacked) — the split disappears and the artifact becomes an inline block below the message that triggered it.

**Heading hierarchy for multi-tab layout:**
Each conversation tab when selected should set a `&lt;h1&gt;` for the conversation title. The artifact pane should use `&lt;h2&gt;` for the artifact title. Never skip heading levels — screen reader navigation depends on this structure.

**Keyboard shortcuts documentation (from Claude Code Official Docs, p. 29):**
Claude Code's keybinding system demonstrates the full catalog of actions needed for a chat planning UI: Chat actions, History actions, Tab actions, Diff actions, Scroll actions, Model picker actions. The lesson: every action accessible via mouse must have a documented keyboard shortcut, surfaced via a `?` shortcut overlay.

---

## Additional Insights

**Dark mode / light mode theming:**
Vercel's hydration-safe pattern prevents flash-of-wrong-theme without React state:
```tsx
&lt;script dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: `(function() {
  var theme = localStorage.getItem('theme') || 'light';
  document.getElementById('root').className = theme;
})();` }} /&gt;
```

**Performance: dynamic imports for the artifact pane:**
```tsx
const ArtifactPane = dynamic(
  () =&gt; import('./ArtifactPane').then(m =&gt; m.ArtifactPane),
  { ssr: false }
);
// Preload on hover of any message that could produce an artifact
function Message({ onMouseEnter }) {
  const preload = () =&gt; { void import('./ArtifactPane'); };
  return &lt;div onMouseEnter={preload}&gt;...&lt;/div&gt;;
}
```

**Using `startTransition` for non-urgent tab switches:**
```tsx
import { startTransition } from 'react';
const handleTabChange = (tabId: string) =&gt; {
  startTransition(() =&gt; setActiveTab(tabId));
};
```
Tab switching is non-urgent — the current tab remains visible while the new one loads, preventing blank-pane flicker.

**`useRef` for streaming token accumulation (Vercel Ch. 5):**
```tsx
const tokenBufferRef = useRef('');
// Updated on every SSE delta without causing re-renders
tokenBufferRef.current += delta.text;
// Flushes to state every 50ms via setInterval for smooth rendering
```
This prevents a re-render on every single SSE token (potentially 20+ per second), batching them into visible chunks while maintaining smoothness.

---

## Citation Summary

**Books read:**
1. UI/UX Design Principles for AI Agents (pp. 3–12, 14)
2. Awesome Design.md (pp. 7, 24, 47, 53 — Claude, Linear, Stripe, Vercel sections)
3. Vercel Frontend Engineering Guidelines (all 4 pages)
4. Claude Code: Official Docs (pp. 335–338)

**Key takeaways:**
Use CSS `transition` (not `@keyframes`) for all interactive state changes — pane open/close, tab switches, badge state — because transitions are interruptible mid-animation · Linear's dark-mode-native palette (`#08090a` canvas, `rgba(255,255,255,0.02–0.08)` card surfaces, `#7170ff` single accent) is the correct reference for a dark-mode planning board with issue cards · Claude Agent SDK streaming uses `includePartialMessages: true` + `inTool` boolean flag to drive live activity indicators without complex state machines · Exits must be 60% the duration of enters and 30–50% the translation distance or the UI appears to argue with the user · `aria-live="polite"` on the streaming text container, focus-trap into the artifact pane on reveal, and keyboard-operable resize handle are the three non-negotiable accessibility requirements · The Doherty Threshold (400ms) governs scheduling control responsiveness — any drag-to-prioritize or date-picker interaction must complete a visible response within 400ms

**Impact:**
These sources go well beyond general React/a11y guidance by providing production-exact token values (HSL palette steps, shadow layer formulas, OpenType feature flags), the precise SDK event sequence for streaming-to-UI (not just "use SSE"), and an explicit animation timing system based on cognitive science (exit = 60% of enter, stagger = 50–100ms, icon swap = 200ms with spring bounce = 0) — none of which appears in general Next.js or shadcn/ui documentation.

**Worth remembering:**
&gt; "510 is the signature weight: Linear uses Inter Variable's 510 weight (between regular 400 and medium 500) as its default emphasis weight. This creates a subtly bolded feel without the heaviness of traditional medium or semibold." — *Awesome Design.md*, Page 24

This is counter-intuitive because the CSS `font-weight` property accepts arbitrary values between 100–900 on variable fonts, but the overwhelming convention is to use 400/500/600/700. Linear's discovery that 510 — splitting the difference between regular and medium — produces a perceptibly different reading rhythm without triggering the "bold signal" in the reader's brain is a rare, production-tested insight that applies directly to the dense text of a multi-tab planning UI where you want information emphasis without visual noise.</result>
